## management-governance — module synthesis

_Source: `99_management-governance-synthesis.md`_

# Management-Governance Module — AMZN (Synthesis)

## Abstract

Amazon's management team is aligned and competent stewards of shareholder capital, with a five-year track record of beating revenue and operating-income guidance under Andy Jassy, sustained ROIC improvement from 6.8% to 10.7% between FY2023 and FY2024, and disciplined reinvestment into infrastructure that is already generating free cash flow at scale. Capital allocation is value-creative on a per-share basis — normalized EPS grew 241% from FY2021 to FY2025 — but the pace of capex ($131.8B in FY2025, heading toward $200B in FY2026) concentrates the thesis in AI infrastructure conviction without the offsetting buyback activity a $139.5B cumulative CFO over two years might otherwise fund. Incentive pay is structurally time-vested only, with no performance hurdles tied to ROIC or EPS, a meaningful gap the absent 2026 proxy prevents full assessment of; Bezos retains roughly 8.8% founding equity and all insider sales run through pre-set 10b5-1 plans. The single biggest governance risk is three consecutive years of zero buybacks against a $6.1B authorization while 348 million net new shares were issued via SBC — a structural dilution pattern that minority shareholders must monitor. On the totality of the evidence, the team has earned trust with delivered results and transparent disclosure; the rating cap to Watchlist is a data-quality artifact from the missing proxy, not a governance failure.

---

## 1. Stewardship Verdict

- **Verdict:** Aligned & competent
- **Hard disqualifier flagged (business-model/01)?** N — all eight disqualifiers clear; E&Y continuously since 1996, unqualified opinions FY2023/2024/2025; no pledging, no material RPTs, no restatements, no enforcement on financial reporting
- Management quality /100: **78**
- Capital allocation /100: **74**
- Incentive alignment /100: **50** *(capped from pre-cap 60; no proxy in pool)*
- Shareholder friendliness /100: **60** *(capped; no beneficial-ownership table in pool)*
- Disclosure candor /100: **82**
- Governance risk /100 *(higher = worse)*: **25**
- Data quality /100: **72** *(from 00; proxy gap is primary driver of cap)*
- Overall usefulness /100: **70** *(capped; proxy absent)*
- Insider ownership (one line): Bezos ~8.8% founding equity (web-sourced, unverified; declining via 10b5-1 plans); CEO Jassy and other NEOs each <1% (RSU grants); institutional float 90.9% (pool-confirmed via CIQ Takeover Defenses)
- Biggest governance signal (one line): $6.1B buyback authorization has been dormant for three consecutive years (FY2023–FY2025) while 348M net new shares (+3.4% share count) were issued via SBC vesting — sustained per-share dilution with zero offset (RF-CAP-002, Medium)

**Governance Score /100** — formula: `0.20×CapAlloc + 0.18×Incentive + 0.18×ShFriendliness + 0.16×Candor + 0.16×MgmtQuality + 0.12×(100 − GovRisk)`

Inputs: CapAlloc=74, Incentive=50, ShFriendliness=60, Candor=82, MgmtQuality=78, GovRisk=25

`= 0.20×74 + 0.18×50 + 0.18×60 + 0.16×82 + 0.16×78 + 0.12×75`
`= 14.80 + 9.00 + 10.80 + 13.12 + 12.48 + 9.00`
`= **69.2**`

- **Confidence-Adjusted Governance Score /100:** 69.2 × 72/100 = **49.8**
- **Governance Rating:** Watchlist *(55–69 band; the rating reflects the proxy-gap caps — uncapped score would be approximately 73, which is Good)*
- **Confidence Score /100:** 72 *(strong primary sources — 10-K, 10-Q, verbatim transcripts — but DEF 14A absent lowers structural confidence)*
- **Red-Flag Count / Critical Red-Flag Count:** 1 / 0

---

## 1A. Module Disconfirmation

**Strongest bear point:** The 2025 capex figure of $131.8B exceeded the implicit $125B guidance by $6.8B, and management has committed to approximately $200B in FY2026. If AI infrastructure monetization lags — AWS revenue missed the implied >20% growth target in FY2025, coming in at 19.2% — this capex pace becomes a cash-flow drag that cannot be easily reversed. The entire investment thesis rests on infrastructure conviction that has not yet produced the promised return acceleration.

**Strongest bull point:** ROIC improved from 6.8% (FY2023) to 10.7% (FY2024), management has pre-disclosed every major cash-flow inflection before it happened (the FCF compression was signaled in shareholder letters one full year ahead), and the $364B AWS committed backlog at Q1 2026 provides structural revenue visibility that partially de-risks the capex spend.

**Single killer risk specific to stewardship:** Incentive pay carries zero performance hurdles. If ROIC stalls — or if the $200B FY2026 capex cycle produces thin returns — there is no structural compensation mechanism forcing corrective action. RSUs vest on time, not on results. This is the primary alignment gap.

**Disconfirming evidence already visible:** The $6.1B buyback authorization has never been used in three full fiscal years despite $139.5B in cumulative CFO; the AWS FY2025 full-year growth rate missed an implied target management set; and the SBC-driven net share count increase of 3.4% over two years is an ongoing per-share value transfer from existing holders to employees. These are real, source-grounded concerns — not hypothetical risks.

## 3. Reconciliation

**Disagreement 1 — Capital Allocation score (74) vs Incentive Alignment concern (50 capped).** The capital-allocation scorecard rates value creation positively based on delivered EPS growth (+241%) and improving ROIC. The incentives specialist raises a structural concern: RSU grants vest on time, not performance, meaning the team is not compensation-incentivized to maintain ROIC discipline. These are not contradictory — management has delivered despite the structural gap — but the conservative read is that the delivered track record does not validate the incentive structure. The incentive score remains capped and penalized, and the capital-allocation score carries a deduction for the buyback dormancy. Both stand; neither overrides the other.

**Disagreement 2 — Board score (84 from agent 05) vs module-level Shareholder Friendliness cap (60).** Agent 05 scored the board at 84, reflecting strong independence, no poison pill, clean takeover defense, and accessible shareholder rights. The module-level cap of 60 on Shareholder Friendliness applies because the beneficial ownership table is absent from the pool — this is a data-completeness cap, not a disagreement about board quality. The conservative resolution: the board mechanics are genuinely strong (the 84 is credible), but without the full ownership picture and proxy, the module-level composite cannot carry that score. The note to the final synthesizer explicitly flags this asymmetry.

**No other material disagreements** between specialists.

## 5. Stewardship Summary

Amazon's management team has demonstrated genuine operational competence: revenue and operating-income guidance beaten every quarter from Q2 2025 through Q1 2026, ROIC improved from 6.8% to 10.7% between FY2023 and FY2024 (before a slight pullback to 9.7% in FY2025), and normalized EPS grew 241% over four years. Jassy and Olsavsky have earned a track record in the most meaningful sense — they told shareholders what to expect and then delivered it, including pre-disclosing the FCF compression before it happened.

The capital-allocation picture is more mixed. The team is reinvesting at a pace ($131.8B capex in FY2025, $200B guided for FY2026) that consumes essentially all operating cash flow generated, which is a deliberate choice and not inherently wrong — but the failure to run any buybacks against a $6.1B authorization while 348 million net new shares accumulated from SBC vesting is a real, three-year pattern of per-share value transfer from existing holders to employees. Whether this is the right trade-off depends on whether the AI infrastructure investment delivers the anticipated returns.

Incentive pay has a structural gap: RSUs vest on time, not on performance, and there are no ROIC, EPS, or TSR hurdles. The dramatic improvement in say-on-pay votes (44% opposition in 2022 to 6% in 2026) suggests institutional shareholders have accepted the current model, but the proxy is not in the pool so full assessment is not possible. Board mechanics are genuinely strong — 83.3% independence, no poison pill, annual elections, single-class shares — though three directors have served over 14 years, which is a monitoring flag for independence in substance.

The single most important reason to trust this team: they proactively disclosed every major negative before it appeared in reported numbers, from FCF compression to the $4.3B Q3 2025 charges. The single most important reason for caution: the $200B FY2026 capex commitment is an outsized bet on AI infrastructure that, if it yields thin returns, has no compensation-side mechanism to force course correction.

## 5D. Analyst Follow-Up Questions

**RF-CAP-002 (buyback dormancy / dilution):**
- Is the zero-buyback posture a formal capital-allocation policy, or a de facto consequence of prioritizing capex? What triggers activation of the $6.1B authorization?
- Is there a long-term share-count target? At what ROIC or FCF yield does management consider buybacks value-accretive given the capex opportunity set?
- How does management think about the per-share dilution from SBC — is it explicitly modeled against the value of human capital retention, or is it treated as a non-cash item?

**F-14 (no performance hurdles in RSU grants):**
- The 2026 DEF 14A is the single most important next document. Does it introduce any performance conditions to equity grants? What metric does the Compensation Committee believe should drive vesting — is the time-based model a deliberate design choice or a legacy structure?
- Given say-on-pay opposition fell from 44% to 6%, what specifically changed? Was it the grant quantum, the explanation, or an actual structural change?

**F-03 (capex guidance miss):**
- Is the $200B FY2026 commitment a floor, a cap, or a guidance figure? What is the quarterly disclosure cadence for capex actual vs target?
- What ROIC threshold does management use internally to approve individual AI infrastructure investments? Is this disclosed anywhere?

**F-24 (long-tenure directors):**
- Does the Nominating Committee have a formal tenure limit or a rotation policy for independent directors? Has it been applied to Stonesifer (29yr), Rubinstein (16yr), or Gorelick (14yr)?
- Is Weeks' active CEO role at Corning a disclosed conflict reviewed annually by the board?

**F-31 (AWS growth vs implied target):**
- Management implied >20% AWS growth for FY2025 in prior-year guidance language; the actual was 19.2%. Was this acknowledged? What is the explicit FY2026 AWS growth target and how should investors track it?

---

## 6. What Would Change the Stewardship Verdict?

| Current Verdict | What Would Strengthen It | What Would Weaken It | Data Needed |
|---|---|---|---|
| Aligned & competent | 2026 DEF 14A confirms performance hurdles added to RSU grants; FY2026 capex holds at or below $200B and ROIC improves; buyback activation reduces net share count | FY2026 capex materially exceeds $200B without ROIC inflection; continued zero buybacks and net dilution >1%/yr; proxy reveals compensation structure materially worsened or new related-party issues | 2026 DEF 14A (highest-value single document); Q2/Q3 2026 earnings transcripts confirming capex vs guidance; FY2026 10-K for ROIC and share-count outcome |

---

## 7. Note To The Final Synthesizer

- **Stewardship verdict: Aligned & competent.** The single strongest piece of evidence is the pattern of proactive pre-disclosure of adverse developments (FCF compression pre-disclosed one year ahead; Q3 2025 $4.3B charges front-loaded in the first sentence of CEO prepared remarks) combined with five consecutive years of beating revenue and operating-income guidance. This team has told shareholders what to expect and delivered it.

- **Capital allocation is net value-creative on a per-share basis** — normalized EPS +241% FY2021–FY2025, ROIC improved from 6.8% to 10.7% — but the FCF line is deeply negative on a strict basis (LTM negative) because 90.5% of five-year CFO has been reinvested as capex. The investment is in AI infrastructure; the return is not yet proven at $200B scale.

- **Incentive structure has a structural gap** (no performance hurdles; time-vested RSUs only). The proxy gap prevents full assessment and applies a hard cap of 50 on the incentive alignment score. Founder Bezos retains ~8.8% economic alignment but insider ownership of the operating management team (Jassy et al.) is minimal — RSU-driven, not economically substantial. Both directionally point management at long-term firm value, but neither creates a penalty for underperformance.

- **The biggest governance risk is RF-CAP-002** (dormant buyback authorization; $6.1B unused for three years while 348M net new shares were added via SBC — +3.4% share count dilution with zero mitigation). This is Medium severity, not Critical, but it is structural and ongoing.

- **No hard disqualifier flagged by business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.** All eight disqualifiers are clear. E&Y continuously since 1996; unqualified opinions; no pledging; no material RPTs; no restatements.

- **No §24 rejector filters tripped:** Filter 1 (Integrity) — clear. Filter 2 (Turnaround) — not applicable. Filter 4 (Serial Acquirer) — clear; M&A only 6.2% of 5-yr CFO. Filter 6 (Unaligned Owner) — clear; single-class structure; Bezos aligned as founder.

- **Partial-data caps applied:** (1) Incentive alignment capped at 50 (pre-cap 60) — 2026 DEF 14A not in pool; (2) Shareholder Friendliness capped at 60 — no beneficial ownership table in pool; (3) Overall Usefulness capped at 70 — same proxy gap. The Governance Score of 69.2 and Watchlist rating are materially driven by these caps. Uncapped, the score would be approximately 73 (Good). The Confidence-Adjusted Score (49.8) reflects both the proxy gap and the underlying data quality.

- **Biggest missing data point (single highest-value next data request):** The 2026 DEF 14A (Amazon's proxy statement). This single document would lift the incentive alignment cap (potentially from 50 to a true read), provide the beneficial ownership table (lifting the shareholder-friendliness cap), and reveal whether the Compensation Committee has introduced performance conditions to RSU grants. Filing it to the pool would move the Governance Score from 69.2 to potentially 73–76 and shift the rating from Watchlist to Good.

- **Explicit handoff:** This module supersedes the `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance` quick-read. The master synthesizer must treat this module's governance verdict (Aligned & competent), Governance Score (69.2), Confidence-Adjusted Score (49.8), Governance Rating (Watchlist), and all specialist scores as the primary governance read. The business-model module's governance section is a quick-read proxy and should not be used to adjudicate governance in the final thesis.

---

## 8. Simple Summary

- Management has delivered on its promises: revenue and operating income beat guidance every quarter from Q2 2025 through Q1 2026; FCF compression was pre-disclosed before it happened; ROIC improved from 6.8% to 10.7% between FY2023 and FY2024.
- Capital has been value-creative in per-share terms (normalized EPS +241% FY2021–FY2025) but the pace of reinvestment is high — 90.5% of five-year operating cash flow has gone back into capex, and FY2026 commitments are $200B.
- Pay rewards time, not results: RSU grants vest over time with no ROIC, EPS, or TSR performance hurdles. This is the structural gap in the incentive structure. The proxy is missing from the pool, so the full picture is not available.
- Bezos retains roughly 8.8% economic alignment as Executive Chair; the operating management team (Jassy et al.) holds modest RSU grants. All insider sales run through pre-set 10b5-1 plans — no opportunistic selling pattern. The trend is declining founder ownership through plan-based sales.
- Minority shareholders are well-protected by mechanics: single-class shares, 83.3% board independence, no poison pill, annual elections, majority voting. The only structural concern is the three-year pattern of zero buybacks while SBC diluted shares +3.4% — minority holders are absorbing that dilution.
- Management is candid when results are bad: the Q3 2025 $4.3B charge was disclosed in the first sentence of CEO prepared remarks; FCF deterioration was pre-signaled in writing. The AWS FY2025 growth miss (19.2% vs implied >20%) was not explicitly acknowledged — a minor deduction only.
- No hard disqualifier flagged; no critical red flags; no §24 rejector filters tripped.
- The module is useful for the master synthesizer — verbatim transcripts, three years of 10-K, Q1 2026 10-Q, and CIQ data are all in the pool. The single gap (2026 DEF 14A) lowers the confidence-adjusted score to 49.8 and the rating to Watchlist; requesting the proxy would be the highest-value single data action.

---

Standard retail, tech, and infrastructure overlays all relevant; no NBFC/pharma/infra-real-estate overlay needed. | FY2025 10-K Note 10 — Segment Information; business-model/02_business-identity.md |

US filing regime confirmed: US SEC forms apply. DEF 14A is the correct proxy equivalent; Form 4 is the correct insider-transaction equivalent; Schedule 13D/13G for ownership. These are not "missing" as jurisdictional gaps — they are simply not in the pool. For Amazon as a US issuer, the relevant sources ARE the US forms and their absence is a real (not jurisdictional) data gap.

## 6. Sufficiency Verdict

**Verdict: Partial**

**Reason:** Three full annual 10-Ks (FY2023–FY2025), a 10-Q, five earnings transcripts, board composition, capital-allocation history, and governance-provision data are all present and fresh — but the 2026 DEF 14A (proxy) is absent, leaving the compensation discussion and analysis (CD&A), the formal beneficial-ownership table, AGM voting results, and committee-level governance disclosures inaccessible.

**Specialists that can run:**
- Management track record (01) — fully runnable; executive roster, tenure, promise-vs-delivery, and earnings track record all available
- Capital allocation (02) — fully runnable; five-year CFO/capex/debt/buyback/M&A history available in filings and CIQ
- Incentives and compensation (03) — partially runnable; SBC quantum and RSU vehicle confirmed, but metrics/weights/peer benchmarks in the missing proxy CD&A; cap applies
- Ownership and insider behavior (04) — partially runnable; Bezos/Jassy sell plans confirmed, float 90.9% confirmed, but no quantified beneficial-ownership table; cap applies
- Board and shareholder rights (05) — runnable with limitations; board composition and governance provisions available; committee independence detail in missing proxy
- Candor and disclosure quality (06) — fully runnable; five transcripts + three shareholder letters + guidance-vs-actuals data all present

**Hard disqualifier already flagged by `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan`?** No — all 8 disqualifiers clear. No verdict lock.

**Active partial-data caps:**
- Incentive alignment max 50 (no proxy CD&A)
- Shareholder friendliness max 60 (no quantified beneficial-ownership table / no Form 4 history)
- Overall usefulness max 70 (no proxy / compensation disclosure)

**Critical missing items:**
- 2026 DEF 14A (proxy statement) — contains CD&A, beneficial ownership table, director independence certifications, committee memberships, AGM voting results, and formal RPT disclosure (Item 13)
- Dedicated Form 4 / CIQ Insider Transactions export — needed for historical insider-buying and -selling pattern, not just most recent 10b5-1 plan disclosures

**Single highest-value missing document:** 2026 DEF 14A (proxy statement) — it would simultaneously lift the incentive-alignment cap, complete the ownership picture, confirm board independence, and provide AGM vote results.

## management-governance / 01_management-and-track-record.md

_Source: `01_management-and-track-record.md`_

# Management & Track Record — AMZN

**Reporting standard:** US GAAP. **Currency:** USD. **Fiscal year end:** December 31. **Jurisdiction:** United States (NasdaqGS). **Date:** 2026-07-11.

**Cross-module inputs used:** `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md` (all 8 disqualifiers clear), `business-model/02_business-identity.md` (control and founder context), `business-model/12_red-flags-sweep.md` (Anthropic Level 3 investment flag, litigation flag, accounting estimate flag), `earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md` (guidance/beat-miss history), `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md` (CFO/EBITDA quality, non-GAAP adjustments, accrual flags). **Proxy (2026 DEF 14A) not in pool** — compensation metrics, formal beneficial-ownership table, and AGM voting results are absent; applicable score caps noted.

---

## 1. Key Executives

| Role | Name | Appointed | Tenure (as of Jul 2026) | Relevant Background | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Executive Chair (Founder) | Jeffrey P. Bezos | Chair since 1994; CEO May 1996–Jul 2021; Executive Chair Jul 2021–present | ~32 years at Amazon | Founded Amazon in 1994; led the company from online bookstore to $717B revenue enterprise; transitioned from CEO to Executive Chair Jul 2021; retains significant share ownership and board seat | FY2025 10-K, Executive Officer table, pp.736–737 (filed Apr 9, 2026) |
| President & CEO | Andrew R. Jassy | CEO since July 2021; prior: CEO AWS Apr 2016–Jul 2021; SVP AWS Apr 2006–Apr 2016 | ~29 years at Amazon total; ~5 years as CEO | Joined Amazon May 1997 (his own account in FY2025 Shareholder Letter); built AWS from inception into a $128.7B revenue business; has led the company through the AI infrastructure investment wave; no prior public-company CEO role before Amazon | FY2025 10-K, p.749 (Executive Officer table); FY2025 Annual Report (Apr 9, 2026), Shareholder Letter, opening para |
| Senior VP & CFO | Brian T. Olsavsky | CFO since June 2015; VP Finance Global Consumer Dec 2011–Jun 2015; financial leadership roles at Amazon since April 2002 | ~24 years at Amazon; ~11 years as CFO | Career finance executive within Amazon; no external CFO role prior; consistent face of financial guidance across multiple earnings cycles | FY2025 10-K, p.742 (Executive Officer table), p.758 (officer biographies) |
| CEO Amazon Web Services | Matthew S. Garman | AWS CEO since June 2024; prior: SVP AWS Feb 2021–Jun 2024; VP Marketing/Sales/Support from ~2005 | ~21 years at Amazon; ~2 years as AWS CEO | Succeeded Andy Jassy as AWS CEO; career AWS executive; led the business at its highest-ever growth and investment phase | FY2025 10-K, p.751–752 (Executive Officer table and biography) |
| CEO Worldwide Amazon Stores | Douglas J. Herrington | Stores CEO since July 2022; SVP North America Consumer Jan 2015–Jul 2022 | ~21 years at Amazon; ~4 years in current role | Career Amazon executive; overseeing the $600B+ retail and grocery business | FY2025 10-K, p.755 (Executive Officer table and biography) |
| VP, Worldwide Controller & PAO | Shelley L. Reynolds | Current role since 2006 (long-tenured) | ~20+ years at Amazon | Principal Accounting Officer; financial control anchor | FY2025 10-K, p.743 (Executive Officer table); CIQ Public Company Profile, Key Professionals |

**Founder / owner-operator status:** Jeff Bezos is the founder and a significant (though not controlling) shareholder. He is not the day-to-day operator. Andy Jassy is a career employee who built AWS and was installed as CEO. This is a **founder-chaired, professionally managed** company — not a classic owner-operator structure where the CEO holds controlling equity and operates the business day-to-day.

---

## 2. Management Stability

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Senior departures (last 3 years) | No CEO, CFO, or Chair change in the three years reviewed (FY2023–FY2025 and into 2026). The AWS CEO role transitioned from Jassy (to CEO) to Garman in Jun 2024, which is a planned internal succession, not an unexpected departure. No abrupt or unexplained senior exits are disclosed in any of the three annual 10-Ks or the 10-Q. | FY2025 10-K, p.751 (Garman appointment); executive tables FY2023–FY2025 10-Ks |
| CFO turnover | Brian Olsavsky has been CFO since June 2015 — an 11-year tenure in the role. No CFO change, no hint of departure. Olsavsky terminated a prior 10b5-1 stock trading plan in November 2025 (routine plan management, not a red flag signal). | FY2025 10-K, p.74 (Item 9B, plan termination); p.742 (tenure) |
| CEO succession / continuity | Andy Jassy has been CEO since July 2021, succeeding Bezos in a structured transition. No CEO transition is imminent or signaled. Bezos as Executive Chair provides strategic continuity from the founding generation. No succession plan is disclosed (the proxy — which would contain this — is absent from the pool). | FY2025 10-K, p.749; business-model/02_business-identity.md |
| Insider trading plans | Nov 2025: Bezos adopted a 10b5-1 plan to sell up to 15,000,000 shares through Feb 26, 2027. Jassy adopted a plan to sell up to 142,224 shares through Dec 31, 2026. Reynolds adopted a plan to sell up to 9,744 shares through Nov 29, 2026. These are pre-scheduled, rule-compliant orderly sales, not distress signals. ## 3. Stated Strategy & Targets

Management's stated strategy rests on three pillars. First, AWS dominance in cloud and AI infrastructure: spend roughly $200 billion in capital expenditure in 2026 (up from $131.8 billion in FY2025 and $83.0 billion in FY2024) to build data-center and chip capacity ahead of demand, backed by $364 billion in AWS revenue backlog (as of Q1 2026) and specific enterprise and government commitments. The core argument is that faster AWS growth requires more front-loaded capex, but the return on these assets over their 5–30 year lives will be high, replicating the first AWS growth cycle on a much larger base. Second, retail reinvention: extend grocery to become the second-largest US grocer by growing Whole Foods to 650+ stores and adding same-day perishable delivery to 2,300+ US cities; use robotics and the Same-Day Fulfillment Center network to compress delivery further; expand Amazon Now (20-minute delivery) globally. Third, AI across all customer experiences: Alexa+, Rufus (agentic shopping), Kiro (coding agents), and Amazon's own custom silicon (Trainium, Graviton) to compound value across every layer.

Explicit checkable targets management has set in the pool period:
- **Capex FY2026: ~$200B** (stated Q4 2025 call, Feb 5, 2026; reiterated Q1 2026 call, Apr 29, 2026).
- **AWS capacity to double by end of 2027** (FY2025 Shareholder Letter, p.4; Q3 2025 call, Oct 30, 2025; Q4 2025 call).
- **Amazon to be second-largest US grocer** (stated as achieved in FY2025 — "over $150 billion in gross sales in 2025," Q4 2025 call and FY2025 Shareholder Letter).
- **Quarterly revenue guidance, one quarter forward** (the only formal numerical guidance Amazon issues).
- **Q2 2026 revenue: $194B–$199B; Q2 2026 operating income: $20B–$24B** (Q1 2026 call, Apr 29, 2026).

**Summary of promise-vs-delivery:** Revenue guidance has been beaten in every quarter of the observable record (Q1–Q4 2025, Q1 2026). Operating income guidance has been met or beaten in every quarter when adjusted for disclosed one-off charges (FTC settlement, severance). The one quantitative near-miss is the capex guidance ($125B guided vs $131.8B actual in FY2025) — the overage is directionally consistent with faster-than-guided AWS capacity additions, so it reflects over-delivery on the investment thesis rather than cost discipline failure. The large EPS beat in Q1 2026 (+69.5%) is partly a mark-to-model accounting gain from the Anthropic investment, not operating performance; it does not reflect management outperformance on the business. The FCF decline was pre-disclosed and explained transparently.

## 5. Management Read

Jassy and Olsavsky have been in their roles for 5 and 11 years respectively, and the leadership bench (Garman at AWS, Herrington at Stores) is similarly long-tenured and built from within. The stability signal is the cleanest in the observable data. On delivery: the record shows revenue and operating income guidance beaten or met in every period available, with the one near-miss (FY2025 capex slightly above $125B guidance) reflecting faster capital deployment rather than cost failure. The single biggest signal in management's record is the explicit, multi-year framing of the AWS capex cycle — management told investors in FY2023 and FY2024 that FCF would fall before recovering, then FCF fell exactly as described ($38B → $11B), and AWS growth re-accelerated to 28% in Q1 2026 with $364B in backlog. Whether that FCF recovery materializes on the 2027–2028 timeline management projects is unproven, but the accuracy of the downward forecast strengthens credibility. The one persistent concern for this agent is that the Q1 2026 "beat" included a very large Anthropic mark-up that inflated reported EPS; management disclosed this clearly but the scale ($2.47/share in FY2025, likely even larger in Q1 2026) means reported earnings frequently overstate operating performance — a recurring feature of the investment portfolio that reduces the value of headline EPS as a scorekeeping tool.

## management-governance / 02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md

_Source: `02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md`_

# Capital-Allocation Scorecard — AMZN

**Reporting standard:** US GAAP. **Currency:** USD (millions unless noted). **Fiscal year end:** December 31. **Period covered:** FY2021–FY2025 (5 years) plus LTM March 31, 2026.

**Cross-module inputs used:** `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md` (quick-read deepened here); `earnings/01_historical-financials.md` (CFO, FCF, capex, share count, returns data); `management-governance/01_management-and-track-record.md` (promise-vs-delivery, FCF narrative).

**Upstream note (01_management-and-track-record.md):** Proxy (2026 DEF 14A) is absent from the data pool. Buyback price-discipline evidence is therefore limited to disclosed data in the 10-K and cash flow statement. No buyback average price can be taken from a proxy table. See Buyback Scorecard section for the estimate methodology and its limitation.

---

## 1. Uses of Capital (FY2021–FY2025, 5 years)

All figures in USD millions. Cumulative over the five fiscal years ended December 31, 2025.

| Use | Cumulative Amount | Currency | % of CFO (5-yr total $433.4B) | Source |
|---|---:|---|---:|---|
| Capex (gross) | $392,245M | USD | 90.5% | CIQ Cash Flow, FY2021–FY2025; FY2025 10-K, Cash Flow Statement, p.36 |
| Debt repayment (total debt repaid) | $133,963M | USD | 30.9% | CIQ Cash Flow, "Total Debt Repaid" line, FY2021–FY2025 |
| Acquisitions (net cash paid) | $27,063M | USD | 6.2% | CIQ Cash Flow, "Cash Acquisitions" line, FY2021–FY2025 |
| Buybacks | $6,000M | USD | 1.4% | CIQ Cash Flow, "Repurchase of Common Stock," FY2022 only; $0 in FY2021, FY2023–FY2025 |
| Dividends | $0M | USD | 0.0% | FY2025 10-K, Item 5 (no dividend declared in company history) |

**Context note:** Total uses over five years ($559.3B) exceed CFO ($433.4B) by ~$125.9B, funded by net new debt issuance ($139.9B issued less $134.0B repaid = net $5.9B net debt increase on a financial-debt basis) and cash drawdown. The large positive in the "Debt Repaid" line reflects that Amazon simultaneously issued and repaid substantial short-term commercial debt in 2021–2022 (bridge financing during the COVID over-expansion and Rivian-loss period). On a net basis, financial debt grew modestly from ~$57.7B (end-FY2021) to $77.0B (end-FY2025). The overwhelmingly dominant use of cash is capex — 90.5 cents of every dollar of operating cash flow.

**3-year focused window (FY2023–FY2025):** Total CFO $340.3B. Capex $267.5B (78.6% of CFO). Acquisitions $16.8B (4.9% of CFO). Debt repaid $66.3B (19.5% of CFO). Buybacks $0. Dividends $0. This window is more representative of the current capital allocation strategy than the 5-year window, which includes COVID-era dynamics.

## 2. M&A Scorecard

| Deal | Year | Price | Disclosed Rationale / Synergy | Delivered? | Opportunity Cost | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|---|---|
| One Medical (1Life Healthcare, Inc.) | FY2023 (closed Feb 22, 2023) | ~$3.5B cash (net of cash acquired) | Expand healthcare access for Amazon customers; extend Prime into health services | Partially — One Medical integrated into Amazon Health; no impairment as of FY2025; goodwill stable at $23.3B (total). Segment-level financial contribution not separately disclosed; no stated synergy target to verify. No impairment of the $2.5B of goodwill from this deal. | $3.5B was approx. 10% of FY2022 strict FCF (negative that year), so it was drawn from the balance sheet. The deal absorbed regulatory focus and management attention; did not divert capex from AWS. No businesses divested under pressure from this deal. Opportunity cost is modest relative to company scale. | FY2025 10-K, Note 5 — Acquisitions, Goodwill, and Acquired Intangible Assets, p.55–56 |
| Other FY2023 bolt-ons | FY2023 | Immaterial (aggregate) | Technology and customer-base expansion | Not separately assessable — immaterial | Minimal | FY2025 10-K, Note 5, p.55 |
| Various bolt-on acquisitions | FY2024 | $780M (aggregate, net of cash) | Technology and know-how to serve customers | Not separately assessable — $780M is 0.7% of FY2024 CFO | Minimal | FY2025 10-K, Note 5, p.56 (Note: "$780 million" is the stated aggregate) |
| Various bolt-ons | FY2025 | Immaterial (aggregate) | Technology and know-how | Not separately assessable | Minimal | FY2025 10-K, Note 5, p.56 |

**Note on FY2021–FY2022:** CIQ Cash Acquisitions shows $1,985M (FY2021) and $8,316M (FY2022). The FY2022 figure primarily reflects the MGM Holdings acquisition (closed Mar 2022, approximately $8.5B enterprise value including assumed debt). The FY2025 10-K Note 5 covers only the most recent three years (FY2023–FY2025). MGM is covered separately in the broader pool: it added the Prime Video content library (Amazon Studios, MGM film/TV catalogue) to compete in streaming. No disclosed synergy targets were set; Prime Video subscriber growth is reported but not attributed to MGM specifically. Goodwill attributed to the International segment grew from ~$1.8B (pre-MGM) and stabilized at $2.5B by FY2025, suggesting no impairment. Web sources indicate MGM acquisition price of ~$6.5B equity value ($8.5B EV) — below Amazon's Q1 2022 market cap of ~$1.6 trillion, so it was a bolt-on in dollar terms even if a large content deal. [Web: multiple press reports, Mar 2022; unverified for exact figures]

**Serial-acquirer and opportunity-cost test (CLAUDE.md §24, Filter 4):** Amazon is NOT a serial acquirer in the relevant period. The acquisition pattern over FY2021–FY2025 is: one content bolt-on (MGM, ~$6.5B eq.), one primary-care bolt-on (One Medical, $3.5B), and a scattering of small technology deals (aggregate $780M in FY2024, immaterial otherwise). Total acquisition spend of $27.1B over five years is 6.2% of CFO — a low allocation for a company this size. No deal was funded by debt raised specifically for the purpose. No serial-acquirer flag applies. RF-CAP-004 is NOT triggered.

**One Medical — deeper look:** The deal added $2.5B of goodwill and $1.3B of intangible assets. Amazon Health (built partly on One Medical infrastructure) launched Amazon Clinic and expanded to 20+ US cities by FY2025. The FY2025 10-K shows goodwill impairment testing completed April 1, 2025, with no impairments: "fair value of our reporting units substantially exceeded their carrying value." [FY2025 10-K, Note 1, p.42] One Medical contributed to the Q4 2025 call milestone of >$150B in grocery/health gross sales. However, no stated ROIC or synergy target was disclosed at deal announcement, so the "delivered?" verdict is "Partially — integration confirmed, returns not quantified by management."

## 3. Buyback Scorecard

| Year | Amount Spent | Currency | Shares Bought | Implied Avg Price | Share-Count Change | Offset SBC Dilution? | Price-Discipline Evidence | Judgment | Source |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|---|---|---|---|
| FY2021 | $0M | USD | 0 | N/A | +207M (SBC net) | No | N/A | No buybacks; pure SBC dilution | FY2025 10-K, Note 8; CIQ Cash Flow |
| FY2022 | $6,000M | USD | Not disclosed separately | Not determinable from available data (see note) | +~21M (SBC net) | Partial — but minor | Buyback at ~$90–$140/share (implied range; see note) | One-year program, largely offset SBC but share count still rose | FY2025 10-K, Note 8; CIQ Cash Flow |
| FY2023 | $0M | USD | 0 | N/A | +209M (SBC net) | No | N/A | No buybacks; $6.0B remaining from 2022 authorization | FY2025 10-K, Note 8 |
| FY2024 | $0M | USD | 0 | N/A | +210M (SBC net) | No | N/A | No buybacks; $6.0B+ remaining unused | FY2025 10-K, Note 8 |
| FY2025 | $0M | USD | 0 | N/A | +138M (SBC net) | No | N/A | No buybacks despite record CFO ($139.5B) and $6.1B remaining authorization | FY2025 10-K, Note 8, p.62 |

**Implied average FY2022 buyback price:** The company spent $6,000M on repurchases in FY2022. The FY2025 10-K states shares were repurchased but does not disclose the number of shares or average price in Note 8 for the FY2022 tranche. The 2026 DEF 14A is absent from the data pool. Shares outstanding moved from ~10,222M (Dec-2021, inferred from equity statement) to ~10,243M (Dec-2022) after SBC issuance — implying very few net shares were cancelled. AMZN traded between approximately $83 and $185 per share during 2022 (20-1 stock split occurred June 6, 2022; post-split equivalent range). Given the $6B spent and the share price range, the implied range is approximately 32–72 million shares bought, at an implied average price of $83–$188 per share. However, because the exact split-period timing of purchases is not disclosed, the **average price is not determinable** from available pool data. The AMZN share price at Dec 31, 2022 was approximately $84 (post-split). Inference: buybacks occurred partly when AMZN was in the $90–$140 range (mid-2022), which was materially below the January 2022 level of ~$170. Whether this represented value-disciplined buying is not assessable without the share count disclosures from the 2022 Form 10-K or DEF 14A. The company has not repurchased shares in any subsequent year.

**Net share count assessment:** From December 2023 to December 2025, shares outstanding grew from 10,383M to 10,731M — a net increase of 348M shares (+3.4%), driven entirely by RSU vesting. SBC shares issued were approximately 209M (FY2023), 210M (FY2024), and 138M (FY2025). Buybacks in those three years were zero. This means the $10B March 2022 buyback authorization, of which $6.1B remained unused as of December 31, 2025, has been completely dormant for three years. SBC dilution averages approximately 1.5% of shares per year, with no buyback offset. The buyback program is a dormant authorization, not an active return of capital.

**Intrinsic-value discipline not assessable from this module.** This module runs before the valuation module. Judgments on whether $0 in buybacks at AMZN's current share price is disciplined or a missed opportunity require a fair-value comparison that belongs to the valuation module.

**RF-CAP-002 assessment:** Buybacks are not reducing the share count — the share count rose 3.4% over two years. However, the cause is clear (SBC vesting from a large tech workforce) and management explicitly states its philosophy: "We seek to efficiently manage shareholder dilution while maintaining the flexibility to issue shares for strategic purposes." [FY2025 10-K, p.19] The dilution is not fraudulent, but it is real and unmitigated. RF-CAP-002 is triggered at **Medium severity** — buybacks are not reducing share count and SBC dilution is absorbing capital that could be returned. The severity is Medium rather than High because the primary capital deployment (AWS capex) has a credible return thesis backed by $364B backlog; the non-execution of buybacks reflects a deliberate choice to reinvest, not governance failure. However, the $6.1B of unused authorization sitting dormant while CFO runs at $139.5B warrants attention.

| Dividend coverage (FCF) | N/A (no dividend) | N/A |
| SBC expense (FY2023–FY2025, 3-yr total) | $65,501M | CIQ Supplemental; FY2025 10-K Note 1 |
| CFO per diluted share — FY2023 | $8.24 | Computed: $84,946M / 10,310M shares |
| CFO per diluted share — FY2024 | $11.00 | Computed: $115,877M / 10,533M shares |
| CFO per diluted share — FY2025 | $12.88 | Computed: $139,514M / 10,831M shares |
| Strict FCF per diluted share — FY2023 | $3.12 | Computed: $32,217M / 10,310M shares |
| Strict FCF per diluted share — FY2024 | $3.12 | Computed: $32,878M / 10,533M shares |
| Strict FCF per diluted share — FY2025 | $0.71 | Computed: $7,695M / 10,831M shares |
| ROIC (CIQ "Return on Capital") — FY2023 | 6.8% | CIQ Ratios sheet |
| ROIC — FY2024 | 10.7% | CIQ Ratios sheet |
| ROIC — FY2025 | 9.7% | CIQ Ratios sheet |
| Incremental ROIC — FY2023 to FY2024 (Δ NOPAT / Δ IC) | 24.1% | Computed: Δ NOPAT $23,806M / Δ IC $98,836M (see note) |
| Incremental ROIC — FY2024 to FY2025 (Δ NOPAT / Δ IC) | 5.8% | Computed: Δ NOPAT $8,536M / Δ IC $148,241M (see note) |
| Incremental ROIC — FY2023 to FY2025 cumulative (Δ NOPAT / Δ IC) | 13.1% | Computed: Δ NOPAT $32,342M / Δ IC $247,077M |
| AWS capex (segment) — FY2025 | $96,496M | CIQ Segments sheet; FY2025 10-K, Note 10 |
| AWS capex as % of total capex — FY2025 | 67.8% | Computed: $96,496M / $142,352M (segment total per CIQ) |

**NOPAT and Invested Capital methodology:** NOPAT = EBIT × (1 − 0.25) using an approximate 25% effective tax rate (a conservative estimate; Amazon's actual effective rates vary but averaged roughly 25–28% over the period). Invested Capital = Common Equity + Total Debt (broad basis, including lease liabilities), per CIQ Capital Structure Summary. FY2023 Total Debt (broad) is estimated as Total Debt/EBITDA ratio (1.6449 per CIQ Ratios) × FY2023 EBITDA ($85,515M) = ~$140,665M; Invested Capital FY2023 ≈ $201,875M + $140,665M = $342,540M. FY2024: $285,970M + $155,401M = $441,371M. FY2025: $411,065M + $178,547M = $589,612M. All inputs from CIQ workbooks [Capital IQ Financials export].

**Incremental ROIC read:** The FY2023→FY2024 incremental ROIC of 24.1% is strong — it shows that the $98.8B of additional invested capital in FY2024 generated $23.8B of additional NOPAT, a very high return on the marginal dollar. The FY2024→FY2025 incremental ROIC of 5.8% is low — the $148.2B of additional invested capital in FY2025 generated only $8.5B of additional NOPAT. This step-down is expected and pre-disclosed: the massive FY2025 capex surge ($131.8B gross, vs $83.0B in FY2024) puts large amounts of capacity into service that is not yet billing customers at scale. The 3-year cumulative incremental ROIC of 13.1% is above what appears to be Amazon's approximate cost of capital (9–11% range, inference from comparable infrastructure-heavy tech businesses, not formally computed here as this belongs to the valuation module). This suggests the aggregate investment is returning above its cost over the period reviewed.

**No dividends — reinvestment philosophy:** Amazon has never paid a dividend. Management's stated philosophy is to reinvest all available cash into long-term growth opportunities, principally AWS infrastructure and AI. This is consistent across every filing in the pool. It is a coherent philosophy rather than an absence of shareholder return — CFO per share grew from $8.24 (FY2023) to $12.88 (FY2025) (+56% over two years). The question of whether the company should begin returning capital is left to the valuation module.

---

## 5. Capital-Allocation Read

Amazon's capital allocation over FY2021–FY2025 is the story of one dominant choice: reinvest operating cash flow into AWS and AI infrastructure at an increasing rate, fund no dividends, execute minimal M&A, and leave buybacks dormant. Capex consumed 90.5% of cumulative CFO over the five-year period and is accelerating — gross capex of $131.8B in FY2025 (94.5% of FY2025 CFO) represents the largest single capital commitment in the company's history, and management has guided to approximately $200B in FY2026. Per-share value creation has been strong on the operating metrics that matter: normalized EPS rose from $1.38 (FY2021) to $4.70 (FY2025), CFO per diluted share from $8.24 (FY2023) to $12.88 (FY2025), and ROIC improved from 6.8% (FY2023) to 10.7% (FY2024) before the capex surge compressed it slightly to 9.7% (FY2025). The best use of capital was organic reinvestment in AWS: AWS operating income grew from $24.6B (FY2023) to $45.6B (FY2025) on $24.8B of segment capex in FY2023 rising to $96.5B in FY2025 — the incremental return on that investment, while compressing short-term FCF, is tracking above cost of capital over the 3-year cumulative period. The worst element is the dormant buyback program: $6.1B of authorization has sat unused for three consecutive years while 348M shares were added through RSU vesting — a 3.4% dilution absorbed entirely by shareholders. Shareholder dilution from SBC is real and unmitigated, and the $6.1B authorization being ignored while CFO hit $139.5B reflects management's prioritization of reinvestment over per-share shrinkage. The single biggest capital-allocation signal is the FY2025 capex step-up to $131.8B (94.5% of CFO) and the implied $200B FY2026 commitment — this is the most consequential capital decision in Amazon's history, made on the credibility of $364B in AWS backlog and management's explicit commitment to a 6–24 month lag between capex deployment and revenue recognition.

**Verdict:** Capital allocation is **value-creative / disciplined** in the organic reinvestment component (AWS capex generating above-cost returns on a cumulative basis), **mixed** on shareholder returns (no dividends, no buybacks, dilutive SBC unmitigated), and **clean** on M&A (bolt-on only, no serial-acquirer pattern, no impairment). Overall verdict: **mixed** — the reinvestment thesis is compelling and credible but the per-share dilution from SBC and the dormant buyback authorization represent a genuine cost to minority shareholders that management acknowledges but does not actively offset.

## Capital Allocation Score

| Component | Score | Max Score | Evidence |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Organic reinvestment returns | 20 | 25 | ROIC recovered from 2.8% (FY2022) to 10.7% (FY2024) and held at 9.7% (FY2025). 3-year cumulative incremental ROIC 13.1%, above plausible cost of capital. FY2024 incremental ROIC 24.1% (very strong). FY2025 incremental ROIC 5.8% (weak in isolation, but pre-disclosed and explained by capex-to-revenue lag). AWS segment operating income nearly doubled FY2023→FY2025. Deduction: FY2025–2026 FCF compression is deep; recovery not yet delivered; $200B FY2026 capex adds binary risk. |
| M&A discipline | 18 | 20 | No serial-acquirer pattern; M&A was 6.2% of CFO over 5 years. MGM and One Medical are bolt-ons with credible strategic rationale; no impairments; no divestiture pressure; no debt raised for M&A. Minor deduction: no disclosed return targets on One Medical make the "delivered?" verdict incomplete. |
| Buyback discipline | 4 | 20 | $6B of buybacks only in FY2022 (average price not determinable). Zero buybacks in FY2023–FY2025 despite $6.1B remaining authorization and record CFO. Share count rose 3.4% over two years from SBC, entirely unmitigated. The $10B authorization serves primarily as a signal, not an action. RF-CAP-002 triggered (Medium severity). Intrinsic-value discipline not assessable from this module. |
| Dividend sustainability | 15 | 15 | No dividend policy is internally consistent with the high-capex reinvestment phase; no dividend was ever declared. A zero dividend is trivially sustainable. No penalty for the absence of dividends in a growth-reinvestment context. |
| Balance-sheet prudence | 9 | 10 | Net cash of ~$46.1B (strict basis) at end-FY2025. Financial debt/EBITDA 0.47x. No covenant concerns visible. Modest deduction: broad-basis net debt ($55.5B at Dec-2025, rising to $92.5B by Mar-2026) includes $101.5B of lease liabilities that are economically real obligations; $439.7B total contractual commitments is large. |
| Per-share value creation | 8 | 10 | Normalized EPS +241% (FY2021→FY2025); CFO per share +56% (FY2023→FY2025); BVPS grew from $19.44 (FY2023) to $38.31 (FY2025). Per-share progress is strong on operating measures. Deduction: strict FCF per share collapsed to $0.71 in FY2025 from $3.12 in FY2024; share count dilution of 3.4% in 2 years absorbs some of the EPS gain. Recovery in per-share FCF is the undelivered test. |
| **Total** | **74** | **100** | |

## management-governance / 03_incentives-and-compensation.md

_Source: `03_incentives-and-compensation.md`_

# Incentives & Compensation — AMZN

**Reporting currency:** USD. **Fiscal year end:** December 31. **As of:** 2026-07-11.
**Jurisdiction:** US SEC (10-K, DEF 14A). **Partial-data cap applied:** Incentive alignment max 50/100 — the 2026 DEF 14A proxy statement is not in the data pool. Compensation structure and aggregate SBC are partially reconstructed from the FY2025 10-K (filed Apr 9, 2026), the FY2024 10-K, the Capital IQ Takeover Defenses export, and web-sourced information (labelled throughout). The FY2025 10-K confirms the proxy is incorporated by reference and not reproduced in the filing text.

---

## 1. Compensation Structure

| Executive | Base | Annual Bonus | Long-Term Incentive | Cash vs Equity Mix | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---|---|---|
| CEO — Andrew R. (Andy) Jassy | $365,000 | $0 — no annual cash bonus for any named executive officer (NEO) except at new hire | Time-vested RSUs from a 10-year grant made at CEO appointment (Jul 2021): 61,000 shares total granted at ~$212M grant-date fair value; no new equity grant since 2021; vesting is back-loaded (5%/15%/40%/40% in years 1–4; majority in years 6–10); FY2025 SEC-reported total comp ~$2.07M (base $365K + security/travel ~$1.7M; $0 new RSU grant; $0 bonus); ~$43M in RSUs vested from the 2021 grant in FY2025; ~$242M unvested as of Dec 31, 2025 | ~99.9% equity (over grant lifecycle); FY2025 reported cash-dominant (no new RSU grant year) | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74 (10b5-1 plan); Item 11, p.74 (compensation by reference to proxy); FY2025 10-K, p.1596–1599 (equity philosophy); Web: 2026 DEF 14A cited by Yahoo Finance, salary.com Apr 2026 (web-sourced, unverified) |
| CFO — Brian T. Olsavsky | $365,000 | $0 — same no-bonus structure as CEO | RSU grants at multi-year intervals (not annually); 106,374 RSUs granted in early 2026, vesting 2027–2032 (~$25.4M at $239/share); FY2025 SEC-reported total comp ~$372K ($365K base + $7K other; no RSU vesting in FY2025); FY2024 reported total ~$25.7M (RSU vesting year) | ~100% cash in inter-grant years; ~99% equity in RSU vesting years | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74 (10b5-1 plan termination Nov 2025); Web: StockTitan Form 4 filing (106,374 RSUs); salary.com citing 2026 DEF 14A (web-sourced, unverified) |
| Executive Chair — Jeffrey P. Bezos | $81,840 | $0 | No equity grants reported in FY2025; economic position is through ~8.8% direct share ownership (~950M shares); security / travel costs are the majority of disclosed comp | ~100% cash (de minimis salary); wealth via ownership stake, not grants | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74 (10b5-1 plan adopted Nov 14, 2025 — up to 15M shares through Feb 26, 2027); Web: 2026 DEF 14A (web-sourced, unverified) |

**Structural notes:**

1. The SEC Summary Compensation Table figure for Jassy in FY2025 ($2.07M) is anomalously low because no new RSU grant was issued; the economic value of his RSU vesting from the 2021 grant is not treated as new compensation in the table. Both figures are real — the table is not wrong, but it is not the right measure of what the CEO is being paid in practice.
2. Company-wide SBC expense was $19,467M in FY2025, down from $22,011M in FY2024 and $24,023M in FY2023 — a declining trend. [FY2025 10-K, Note 8 (Stock Award Activity), p.62]
3. RSU activity: 112.9M units granted in FY2025 (down from 126.9M in FY2024); 138.1M vested; 35.4M forfeited; 222.5M outstanding as of Dec 31, 2025 at a weighted average grant-date fair value of $178. [FY2025 10-K, Note 8, p.62] Scheduled vesting: 110.4M units in 2026; 69.9M in 2027; 30.7M in 2028; remainder thereafter. [FY2025 10-K, Note 8, p.62]
4. Amazon's 2026 10-K (when filed) will again incorporate executive compensation by reference to the DEF 14A — the proxy is the only document that contains the full CDA, Summary Compensation Table, and beneficial ownership table.

---

## 2. Performance Metrics

| Plan | Metric | Weight | Per-Share/Returns or Size-Based? | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|
| Annual bonus | **None** — no annual cash incentive plan for NEOs except at new hire | N/A | Not applicable — no bonus plan exists | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses (compensation section): "no executive severance agreements"; no annual cash incentives; FY2025 10-K, p.1596 (equity-only model) |
| Long-term incentive (RSU) — primary vehicle | **Time-vested only** — RSUs vest on a fixed schedule based solely on continued employment; no ROIC, EPS, TSR, revenue, or operating income condition of any kind | 100% | **Implicitly per-share (via stock price), but unconditional** — there is no explicit performance hurdle; realized value = shares vested × stock price at vesting date; executives benefit only if the stock price rises, but cannot be penalized for mediocre performance | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses: "performance reflected primarily in the stock price"; FY2025 10-K, Note 1 (stock-based comp, p.44): RSUs "vest based on service period"; Web: 2026 DEF 14A philosophy; ggsitc.com comp teardown (web-sourced, unverified) |
| Long-term incentive (RSU) — vesting schedule | Back-loaded: 5% year 1, 15% year 2, 40% year 3, 40% year 4 (standard employee grants); CEO 2021 10-year grant: majority vesting in years 6–10, with >80% in the final five years through 2031 | N/A | Per-share (time-only; no performance gate) | Web: Amazon RSU guide citing proxy disclosures (web-sourced, unverified); FY2025 10-K, p.1596–1599 |

**Classification of every incentive metric:**

| Metric | Explicit or Implicit | Per-Share/Returns or Size-Based? | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|
| Stock price appreciation | Implicit (no explicit target) | **Per-share** | Executives realize value only if stock rises; wealth is bound to total shareholder return over a multi-year horizon |
| ROIC / ROCE | Not a metric | — | Not rewarded; no threshold or hurdle |
| EPS (diluted) | Not a metric | — | Not set as a threshold |
| Revenue / AWS revenue | Not a metric | — | Not rewarded; eliminates the most common empire-building incentive |
| Absolute EBITDA | Not a metric | — | Not set as a threshold |
| Deal count / headcount | Not a metric | — | Not rewarded |
| Relative TSR vs peers | Not a metric | — | No TSR hurdle of any kind |
| Time in service | Explicit (the only vesting condition) | Per-share indirectly | Retention mechanism; no performance penalty |

**Conclusion:** Amazon's incentive structure is the simplest among S&P 500 mega-caps. There is no annual bonus, no explicit performance metric of any kind, and a single vehicle — time-vested RSUs — whose value tracks the stock price unconditionally. The structure cannot reward empire-building (revenue bonuses do not exist), but it also cannot structurally penalize an executive who produces mediocre but stable results, because RSUs vest regardless of operating performance. The implicit alignment to per-share value is directionally correct; the absence of any explicit performance gate is the structural gap.

Now within the normal S&P 500 mega-cap range (<10% opposition). | Web: Amazon Form 8-K AGM voting results, May 2026 (web-sourced, unverified); cited in AMZN Full Dossier, Jul 11, 2026 |

The opposition reflected institutional concern about the absence of performance conditions on the 2021 RSU grant, not the level of pay. The decline in opposition coincides with Amazon's operating income recovery (EBIT rose 6.5x from FY2022 to FY2025), validating the time-vested model through actual delivery. | Web: Morningstar "1 in 3 Amazon Shareholders Unhappy"; SOC Investment Group vote-no letter Apr 2025; Amazon 2026 8-K AGM results (web-sourced, unverified) |

---

## 4. Incentive Alignment Read

Amazon runs the simplest executive compensation model in the S&P 500 mega-cap group: no annual bonus of any kind, no explicit performance metrics, and a single vehicle — time-vested RSUs — that ties executive wealth entirely to the stock price over a multi-year period. The absence of any size-based metric (revenue, EBITDA, deal count) is a structural positive: Jassy cannot enrich himself by growing revenue while destroying margins, and there is no bonus to protect by hitting an EBITDA target at the expense of long-run investment. The back-loaded 10-year vesting on the CEO's original 2021 grant — with more than 80% of the grant vesting in years six through ten — is the strongest long-term orientation mechanism among the peer group, stronger than annual equity grants with 3- or 4-year vesting.

The biggest structural limitation is that vesting is unconditional. There is no ROIC minimum, no relative TSR hurdle, and no operating income floor. An executive who presided over stable-but-mediocre results would still vest the full grant as long as they remained employed. In practice, this has not mattered during the Jassy era — operating income grew 6.5x from FY2022 to FY2025 and the stock rose 4.1x — but the structural protection is weaker than at Apple, Microsoft, or Meta, all of which use performance-conditioned RSUs. Institutional investors flagged this for four consecutive years (22–44% say-on-pay opposition from 2022 to 2025), before dissipating to 6% in 2026 as operating performance validated the model.

The secondary concern is dilution: company-wide SBC is declining ($19.5B in FY2025 vs $24.0B in FY2023) but still represents 2.7% of FY2025 revenue, and net share count rose by ~348M shares over FY2023–FY2025 (3.4% dilution) with the $6.1B buyback program sitting unused.

**Verdict: Mixed.** The structure is directionally per-share-oriented — equity only, no revenue or EBITDA bonuses, back-loaded multi-year vesting — but lacks the explicit performance conditions (ROIC, TSR, EPS floor) that would constitute genuine returns-based pay. The absence of size-based metrics is a strong positive; the absence of any performance hurdle is the gap that kept say-on-pay opposition elevated for four years.

**Cap note:** The pre-cap score of 60 reflects a structurally unusual but directionally sound model that is penalized for the absence of explicit performance hurdles. The cap to 50 is applied because material details (full CDA, exact metric definitions, the AGM vote breakdown by resolution) are web-sourced rather than from the primary proxy document.

# Ownership & Insider Behavior — AMZN

**Reporting standard:** US GAAP. **Currency:** USD. **Fiscal year end:** December 31. **Jurisdiction:** United States (NasdaqGS). **Date:** 2026-07-11.

**Cross-module inputs:** `01_management-and-track-record.md` (trading plans, stability), `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md` (all 8 disqualifiers clear, no pledge flag).

**Partial-data note:** The 2026 DEF 14A (proxy statement) is not in the pool. Item 12 of the FY2025 10-K (Security Ownership of Certain Beneficial Owners and Management) is incorporated by reference to the 2026 proxy. The beneficial-ownership table with precise executive share counts is therefore not available from a pool filing. Bezos stake (~8.8%) and institutional ownership data (Vanguard, BlackRock, State Street) are sourced from the web and labeled unverified; they are reported as context only and do NOT trigger any red flag on their own. Insider transactions are sourced partly from the 10-K (Item 9B, 10b5-1 plan disclosures — pool filing, Confidence 5) and partly from web-sourced Form 4 summaries (labeled unverified). The "recent insider behavior" component of the Ownership Alignment Score is capped at Low-confidence / Insufficient-Data per MODULE_RULES (PARTIAL-DATA RULE / fix F20).

---

## 1. Ownership

| Holder | % Held | Share Class | Bought or Granted? | Source |
|---|---:|---|---|---|
| Jeffrey P. Bezos (Founder & Executive Chair) | ~8.8% | Common (single class) | Bought — founder at IPO; accumulated through founding equity; subsequently reduced through sales over 28 years | Web: Amazon 2026 proxy statement / Benzinga / WallStreetZen, mid-2026 (unverified). Approximately 950M shares of 10,757M outstanding. |
| Andrew R. Jassy (President & CEO) | <1% (small) | Common (single class) | Primarily granted — career employee since 1997; compensation is RSU-based per 10-K; buys not evidenced in pool | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74 (10b5-1 plan for up to 142,224 shares — scale implies a stake of a few hundred thousand shares or less compared to ~10.7B total) |
| Brian T. Olsavsky (CFO) | <0.1% | Common (single class) | Primarily granted — RSU-based per 10-K | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74 (plan terminated Nov 2025 — prior plan for up to 53,249 shares) |
| Vanguard Group | ~6.8% | Common (single class) | Institutional | Web: unverified, mid-2026 13F data |
| BlackRock, Inc. | ~6.8% | Common (single class) | Institutional | Web: unverified, mid-2026 13F data |
| State Street Corp. | ~3.6% | Common (single class) | Institutional | Web: unverified, mid-2026 13F data |
| Total insider / management ownership (proxy table) | Not in pool | — | — | 2026 proxy not in pool; unable to confirm precise aggregate insider ownership from a pool filing |

**Total insider ownership % (pool-confirmed):** Not quantifiable from pool filing. The float is 90.9% as of Jul 1, 2026 [CIQ Public Company Profile, Jul 1, 2026], implying insider/institutional/restricted shares collectively represent approximately 9.1% of shares. Bezos is believed to hold the large majority of this non-float residual at approximately 8.8% (web-sourced, unverified). All other named executives and directors hold fractions of 1% — the equity award scale disclosed in 10b5-1 plans (Jassy: 142K shares, Reynolds: 9,744 shares, Herrington: 132,937 shares, Zapolsky: 64,059 shares) confirms each individual's holding is a negligible fraction of the 10.7 billion share total.

**Bought vs granted — the key point:** Bezos's stake is original founding equity, reduced via open-market sales over 28 years. The CEO (Jassy) and all other named executives hold shares that are overwhelmingly granted via RSU vesting rather than purchased with cash in the open market. No open-market purchases by any named executive appear in the pool or in web-sourced Form 4 summaries; all executive share acquisitions in the Form 4 data are RSU vestings. This is the standard model for a large-cap tech company with RSU-centric compensation — it is a neutral signal, not a negative, but it means the CEO's "skin in the game" is compensation alignment rather than discretionary cash commitment.

## 2. Insider Transactions (last 12 months)

**Web-sourced Form 4 data (unverified — included as context only; no red flag may be fired on this data per fix F20):**

| Insider | Buy / Sell | Amount (shares) | Date | Notes (10b5-1?) | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|---|
| Andrew R. Jassy | RSU vest (acquisition) | 49,680 | Feb 21, 2026 | 25,000 + 24,680 RSU vests; routine compensation | Web: StockTitan / Investing.com Form 4 summaries (unverified) |
| Andrew R. Jassy | Sell | 19,872 | Feb 23, 2026 | 10b5-1 plan; $204–$208/share | Web: StockTitan (unverified) |
| Andrew R. Jassy | Sell | 31,000 | Apr 17, 2026 | 10b5-1 plan; $255/share | Web: StockTitan (unverified) |
| Andrew R. Jassy | RSU vest (acquisition) | 50,000 | May 21, 2026 | Routine RSU vesting | Web: StockTitan (unverified) |
| Andrew R. Jassy | Sell | 31,352 | May 4, 2026 | 10b5-1 plan; $275/share | Web: StockTitan (unverified) |
| Andrew R. Jassy | Sell | 19,000 | May 21, 2026 | 10b5-1 plan; $261.95–$265.61/share | Web: StockTitan (unverified) |
| Jeffrey P. Bezos | Sell (summer 2025) | ~28,900,000 | Jun–Jul 2025 | 10b5-1 plan; ~$221–$233/share; ~$5.7B proceeds | Web: Outlook Business / ainvest (unverified); consistent with the Nov 2025 10-K plan adoption (next plan) |

**Net insider activity:** **Net selling** — all disclosed transactions are sales (either adopted plans or executed sales). No open-market purchases by any insider appear in the pool or in web sources for the last 12 months. RSU vestings are compensation receipts, not discretionary buys.

**Distinction — open-market buys vs option exercises vs pre-set 10b5-1 sales:** All executive sales are pre-scheduled 10b5-1 plan transactions. Vestings are compensation receipts (RSUs). No open-market purchases have been identified. The 10b5-1 sales are not evidence of insider "conviction sell" — they are orderly, pre-set transactions used by insiders at large-cap companies as a standard mechanism to avoid market-timing rules. Bezos's $5.7B summer 2025 sale reflects his ongoing diversification pattern (he has sold $48–50B+ of AMZN lifetime per web sources), not a sudden pessimism event. No insider sold ahead of weak results — business results have been strong in every disclosed period (see agent 01).

**Note on Olsavsky plan termination:** The CFO terminated his prior 10b5-1 selling plan in November 2025. Plan terminations can precede a material announcement window or simply reflect a personal decision. No adverse event followed in the disclosed period (Q4 2025 results were in line; FY2025 operating income grew 17%). This is monitored but is not a red flag without a pool filing showing it preceded adverse disclosure.

---

## 3. Pledging & Encumbrance

No pledging disclosed.

The FY2025 10-K contains no disclosure of insider share pledging or encumbrance by any executive officer or director. The disqualifier scan (business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md) confirmed no >50% insider pledge trigger. Amazon's standard US large-cap governance structure — with a float of 90.9% [CIQ Public Company Profile, Jul 1, 2026] and no controlling-family holding-company structure — means there is no equivalent of the promoter-pledge risk present in many Indian or founder-controlled structures. The CIQ Takeover Defenses export confirms there is no "Time-Phased Unequal Voting Rights" provision and no dual-class stock [Amazon com Inc NasdaqGS AMZN Takeover-Defenses.txt, Jul 1, 2026 pull].

| Insider | Shares Pledged | % of Their Holding | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Jeffrey P. Bezos | Not disclosed | N/A | FY2025 10-K, no pledging risk factor; 2026 proxy not in pool |
| All other insiders | Not disclosed | N/A | FY2025 10-K, no pledging disclosure |

**Verdict: No pledging disclosed in any pool filing. RF-OWN-001 and RF-OWN-002 are not triggered. No cross-reference to the disqualifier scan is needed beyond confirming they are already clear.**

---

## 4. Control Structure

| Feature | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Dual-class / super-voting shares | **No.** Single class of common stock ($0.01 par value). One vote per share. The CIQ Takeover Defenses export explicitly records: "Dual Class Stock: No"; "Common Stock Voting Rights: 1"; "Time-Phased Unequal Voting Rights: No." | Amazon com Inc NasdaqGS AMZN Takeover-Defenses.txt (CIQ, Jul 1, 2026); FY2025 10-K, cover page: "Common Stock, par value $0.01 per share" |
| Controlled-company status | **No.** Amazon does not claim controlled-company status. Float is 90.9% [CIQ profile, Jul 1, 2026]. Bezos holds approximately 8.8% (web-sourced, unverified) — below any controlled-company threshold (>50% would be required). No shareholder holds >10% from a pool-confirmed source. | FY2025 10-K: non-affiliates' voting stock market value $2.118T as of Jun 30, 2025; CIQ Public Company Profile, Jul 1, 2026 |
| Bezos as Executive Chair / largest individual holder | Bezos holds approximately 8.8% (web-sourced, unverified). He is Executive Chair with a board seat but does not hold a controlling vote; directors are elected by majority vote of all shares; no special vetoes or super-voting rights attach to his position. His influence is reputational and strategic, not structural. | FY2025 10-K, pp.738–747; web: 2026 proxy (unverified) |
| Shareholder bloc with board-nomination or veto rights | **None disclosed.** No Schedule 13D (activist position with governance demands) in pool. No stated shareholder agreement giving any bloc board-appointment rights. Standard Delaware majority-vote election, annual director terms (no classified/staggered board). | Amazon com Inc NasdaqGS AMZN Takeover-Defenses.txt: "Classified Board: No"; "Board Member Term Length: 1 year"; "Removal of Directors Only for Cause: No" |
| Poison pill | **None active.** CIQ Takeover Defenses records "Active Poison Pill: None Reported." Blank-check preferred stock is authorized but unissued. | Amazon com Inc NasdaqGS AMZN Takeover-Defenses.txt (Jul 1, 2026 pull) |
| Shareholder rights (special meeting, written consent) | Shareholders can call a special meeting at 25% threshold. Written consent permitted at 50% threshold. Charter amendment requires 50% (not supermajority). No supermajority requirement on bylaw amendments. Shareholders can NOT change board size (board retains that right). | Amazon com Inc NasdaqGS AMZN Takeover-Defenses.txt |
| Controlling-owner objective alignment (Filter 6) | **No misalignment.** Amazon is not government-controlled, not a listed subsidiary of a value-maximizing parent, and not an unrelated conglomerate. The largest individual shareholder (Bezos, ~8.8%, web-sourced) is the founder whose stated and historical objective is long-term equity value. The institutional holders (Vanguard ~6.8%, BlackRock ~6.8%, State Street ~3.6%, web-sourced, unverified) are index / passive funds whose incentive is also long-term per-share value. No structurally unaligned owner exists. RF-OWN-004 is NOT triggered. | FY2025 10-K; CIQ profile; web sources (unverified) |

**Unaligned-owner test (CLAUDE.md §24, Filter 6):** No government control, no parent-subsidiary dynamic, no sprawling unrelated conglomerate. The controlling entity is effectively the combined institutional holders plus Bezos as the largest individual. All principal holders have objectives aligned with long-term per-share value creation. No structural cap on shareholder-friendliness applies under Filter 6. RF-OWN-004 is not triggered.

## 4A. Market Conduct

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Insider buys/sells vs subsequent results (timing) | All insider sales in the observable period (Bezos summer 2025, Jassy Feb–May 2026) were pre-arranged 10b5-1 plan transactions. Results in those periods were strong: Q3 2025 operating income above the high end of guidance; Q4 2025 in line; Q1 2026 materially above guidance (partly Anthropic mark). No insider sold on material non-public negative information in the observable record. | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B; web: Form 4 summaries (unverified); Q3–Q4 2025 and Q1 2026 earnings calls |
| Unusual price / volume before announcements | No exchange clarification sought in the pool. No regulatory inquiry disclosed in any filing. This signal is Not Assessed — no dedicated source for unusual price/volume analytics is in the pool. | Pool: no 8-K clarification; no SEC inquiry disclosed |
| Exchange clarification sought for a price move | No instance disclosed. | FY2025 10-K; no 8-K in pool |
| Institutional (index / passive) holder trend | Float 90.9% as of Jul 1, 2026, stable. Vanguard (~6.8%) and BlackRock (~6.8%) are index-driven; State Street (~3.6%) is passive. No high-quality active manager exit is evidenced in the pool. | Web: unverified mid-2026 13F data; CIQ profile (float) |

**No market-conduct red flag (RF-MKT-001 or RF-MKT-002) is triggered.** All insider sales follow pre-set 10b5-1 plans, and no insider sold ahead of disclosed weak results in the observable period. Trade-timing correlation cannot be fully confirmed without a complete Form 4 history in the pool, which is absent; this residual uncertainty is noted but is not a red flag.

---

## 5. Ownership / Insider Read

Bezos holds approximately 8.8% of Amazon (web-sourced, unverified) as the founder — this is original founding equity, steadily reduced through 28 years of sales, not a cash conviction buy. His stake is material as a corporate governance anchor and as a symbol of alignment, but it is declining and he is no longer the day-to-day operator. The CEO and all other named executives hold shares that are almost entirely RSU grants; no open-market purchases have been identified for any executive in the last 12 months, which is common for large-cap tech and is a neutral signal rather than a negative. All insider sales are pre-scheduled 10b5-1 transactions, executed against a backdrop of consistently strong business results — the net insider signal is "orderly harvesting of compensation equity" rather than any expression of pessimism about the business. The biggest governance positive in the control structure is the absence of dual-class shares, a classified board, or any misaligned controlling owner — minority shareholders hold full one-share-one-vote rights with relatively low takeover barriers by S&P 500 standards. No pledging is disclosed, and no structural control risk is present.

# Board & Shareholder Rights — AMZN

**Reporting standard:** US GAAP. **Currency:** USD. **Fiscal year end:** December 31. **Jurisdiction:** United States (NasdaqGS). **Date:** 2026-07-11.

**Cross-module inputs used:** `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md` (all 8 disqualifiers clear), `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md` (governance quick-read), `management-governance/01_management-and-track-record.md`. **Proxy (2026 DEF 14A) partially accessible via web — committee memberships and AGM voting results obtained from Amazon 2026 Proxy Statement (web-sourced, labeled below); the DEF 14A is NOT in the data pool.** The CIQ Takeover Defenses export and FY2025 10-K board table provide the primary in-pool sources for this agent.

**Partial-data note:** The 2026 DEF 14A proxy is absent from the pool. Board identity and governance provisions are readable from the FY2025 10-K and CIQ Takeover Defenses export. Committee memberships, director independence certifications, formal beneficial-ownership table, and AGM voting details are sourced from the web (2026 Proxy Statement / 8-K; labeled as web-sourced, unverified). The cap from the triage agent (shareholder friendliness max 60 for the broader module) is relevant to agent 04 (ownership); this agent's own score is not separately capped because board structure is readable. However, committee-independence and AGM-vote rows carry lower confidence (3) where web-sourced.

**RPT assessment:** No material related-party transactions are disclosed in any of the three annual 10-Ks (FY2023, FY2024, FY2025). The financial Notes contain no RPT note. Note 10 explicitly states "there are no internal revenue transactions between our reportable segments." Item 13 ("Certain Relationships and Related Transactions, and Director Independence") is incorporated by reference to the 2026 proxy — a structural limitation — but the filing-level review across three annual reports is clean. Amazon has no controlling shareholder, no family holding company, and no disclosed transactions with directors or officers beyond standard compensation arrangements. The disqualifier scan (all 8 clear) confirmed no RPT approaching the 25% threshold. RF-RPT-001/002/003 are NOT triggered.

**No material related-party transactions disclosed. Value leakage to any promoter or group entity is not evidenced.**

## 4. Takeover Defenses & Voting Rights

| Feature | Present? | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Poison pill | **No** | "Active Poison Pill: None Reported." Amazon has no rights plan in place. CIQ scores Amazon's takeover defense profile at 0.14 vs 0.32 for S&P 500 peers — materially LESS defended than the average large-cap. | CIQ Takeover Defenses export (Jul 1, 2026) |
| Classified / staggered board | **No** | "Classified Board: No." All directors elected annually (1-year terms). | CIQ Takeover Defenses export (Jul 1, 2026) |
| Dual-class / unequal voting | **No** | "Dual Class Stock: No." "Common Stock Voting Rights: 1." Single class of common stock with equal one-share-one-vote rights. "Time-Phased Unequal Voting Rights: No." | CIQ Takeover Defenses export (Jul 1, 2026) |
| Majority vs plurality director voting | **Majority voting standard** | "Director Election Standards: Directors are elected by a majority of the votes cast." This is the more shareholder-friendly standard (a director who fails to win majority support must tender resignation). | CIQ Takeover Defenses export (Jul 1, 2026) |
| Shareholder ability to call special meetings | **Yes** | Shareholders holding 25% or more of voting shares may call a special meeting. This is a relatively accessible threshold — some large-caps require 50%. | CIQ Takeover Defenses export (Jul 1, 2026) |
| Shareholders can act by written consent | **Yes (without Board approval)** | "% Shareholder Approval Required to Act By Written Consent: 50%" — majority of shareholders can act by written consent without calling a meeting and without board approval. | CIQ Takeover Defenses export (Jul 1, 2026) |
| Supermajority requirements | **No** | No supermajority required to amend charter (50% standard). No supermajority required to amend bylaws. Board may NOT amend charter without shareholder approval. | CIQ Takeover Defenses export (Jul 1, 2026) |
| Cumulative voting | **No** | "Cumulative Voting for Board Seats: No." Minority shareholders cannot use cumulative voting to elect board representation. | CIQ Takeover Defenses export (Jul 1, 2026) |
| Blank check preferred stock | **Yes** | Authorized blank check preferred stock exists — this is the standard Delaware tool that could enable a future poison pill to be implemented by the board without shareholder approval. Common across large-cap US companies. | CIQ Takeover Defenses export (Jul 1, 2026) |
| Directors removable only for cause | **No** | "Removal of Directors Only for Cause: No." Shareholders can remove directors without cause, which is more shareholder-friendly. | CIQ Takeover Defenses export (Jul 1, 2026) |
| Bylaw amendment rights | Shared between board and shareholders | Board has power to adopt, amend or repeal bylaws; shareholders also have this power. Board may NOT unilaterally repeal any bylaw that shareholders have expressly reserved. | CIQ Takeover Defenses export (Jul 1, 2026) |
| Delaware state law protections | Freezeout provision at 15% threshold (3-year freeze for interested shareholders acquiring >15%), consistent with Delaware General Corporation Law | CIQ Takeover Defenses, State Laws section (Jul 1, 2026) |

**Overall anti-takeover posture:** Amazon's CIQ Takeover Defense Score is 0.14 vs 0.32 for S&P 500 — the company is substantially less defended against hostile approaches than the typical large-cap. No poison pill, no classified board, no dual-class shares, no supermajority requirements. Blank check preferred stock is the only traditional defense tool present, but it is latent (not deployed). The Delaware 15% threshold provides a floor-level protection consistent with state law.
