| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| 2026 AGM: Director votes | All 10 nominees received majority support. Gorelick received approximately 92.8% support (votes for: 7,298,413,009; withheld: 566,479,319 — approximately 7.2% withheld, below the 20% red-flag threshold). Cooper received approximately 97.2% support (withheld: 221,722,802 — approximately 2.8% withheld). A "vote no" campaign from shareholder groups targeted Rubinstein, Gorelick, Ng, and Stonesifer (Nominating Committee members/former members) for board accountability concerns; Gorelick and Ng received 91.8% and 93.65% independent support respectively. No nominee failed the majority test. | Web: Amazon 8-K voting results filed after May 20, 2026 AGM (unverified); Web: Investor Advocates for Social Justice AGM results reporting (unverified) |
| 2026 AGM: Say-on-pay vote | Advisory vote on executive compensation received approval: 7,391,737,243 votes for, 470,466,853 votes against, 26,155,268 abstentions — approximately 93.8% support vs approximately 6.0% against (below the 20% threshold that would trigger a red flag). | Web: Amazon 2026 AGM 8-K results (unverified) |
| 2026 AGM: Auditor ratification | Ernst & Young ratified as independent auditor for FY2026 — strong shareholder support (specific % not obtained). | Web: Amazon 2026 AGM results summaries (unverified) |
| 2026 AGM: Shareholder proposals | None of the shareholder proposals received majority approval. Proposals included: report on charitable partnerships, data center climate impact disclosure, climate commitment cost disclosure, independent board chair mandate, AI/automation worker advisory council. The highest-profile AI-related proposal received approximately 18.4% support (data center/climate) — below any action threshold. | Web: Glass Lewis "A Look at 2026 Big Tech Shareholder Proposals" (Jul 2026, unverified); Web: AGM results summaries (unverified) |

Boardroom Alpha flagged elevated vote pressure for Cooper and Gorelick pre-meeting. Post-vote, the "vote no" campaign against Nominating Committee members achieved notable but sub-20% opposition. | Web: Boardroom Alpha 2026 Annual Meeting page (unverified) |
| Institutional opposition | A coalition of shareholders (including Investor Advocates for Social Justice, SOC Investment Group) ran a withhold campaign against Nominating Committee members on governance accountability grounds. Elevated opposition (7.2% on Gorelick) was below 20% and does not trigger RF-SHR-001 but is a monitoring signal. | Web: Investor Advocates for Social Justice AGM commentary (unverified) |
| Dilution history | Shares outstanding rose from 10,383M (Dec 2023) → 10,593M (Dec 2024) → 10,731M (Dec 2025) — net increase of ~348M shares (+3.4%) over two years from RSU vesting. The $10B buyback program authorized in March 2022 remains $6.1B unexecuted as of FY2025. Net dilution rate: approximately 1.3% per year. No preferential allotment, QIP, or warrant issuances. SBC of $19.5B in FY2025 is the source of dilution. | FY2025 10-K, Consolidated Statements of Stockholders' Equity, p.40; Note 8 — Stockholders' Equity, p.62; `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md` |
| Director stock ownership guidelines | Present: "Directors Subject to Stock Ownership Guidelines: Yes" | CIQ Takeover Defenses export (Jul 1, 2026) |
| Executive severance / golden parachutes | "Executive Severance Agreements: No." "Change of Control Agreement with CEO: No." "Golden Parachutes: No." | CIQ Takeover Defenses export (Jul 1, 2026) |

**Voting conclusions:** All directors cleared majority votes at the 2026 AGM. The highest withhold percentage (Gorelick at approximately 7.2%) was notably below the 20% threshold that would constitute a red flag (RF-SHR-001). The say-on-pay vote received approximately 93.8% support — very high, suggesting shareholders are not yet alarmed by SBC levels. RF-SHR-001 and RF-SHR-002 are NOT triggered for 2026.

---

## 5. Minority-Shareholder Protection Read

The board provides real if imperfect protection for minority holders. The structural features are genuinely shareholder-friendly: no classified board (annual director elections), no dual-class shares, majority voting standards for director elections, a low 25% threshold to call special meetings, written-consent rights, no supermajority charter-amendment requirements, no poison pill, no golden parachutes. The absence of anti-takeover devices means the company is more susceptible to hostile bids than peers, but it also means the board cannot easily entrench itself against shareholder will. The Lead Independent Director (Gorelick, 14-year tenure) and the split Chair/CEO structure provide formal independence architecture.

The biggest entrenchment risk to minority holders is not structural — it is the very large SBC expense ($19.5B in FY2025, roughly $1.70 per share per year) running through an unused $10B buyback program that has not offset a single share of dilution in 2023, 2024, or 2025. Minority shareholders are experiencing net dilution of approximately 1.3% per year while management's equity compensation continues to grow. This is a capital-allocation concern (owned by agent 02) rather than a governance-structure concern, but it is the clearest direct cost to minority holders from the current governance configuration. The disqualifier scan returned all 8 checks clear; the RF-OWN-004 filter does not apply (no structurally misaligned controlling owner); Bezos as a non-controlling founding shareholder has interests broadly aligned with per-share value creation, as both his wealth and his 10b5-1 sell-plan price depend on the stock.

Verdict: **Adequate** — board structure is sound and minority-friendly features are above the large-cap norm, but ongoing SBC dilution with no buyback offset is the most direct minority-holder cost and the audit committee's independence and financial expertise require continued monitoring (proxy-sourced data, unverified).

[Web: 2026 AGM 8-K results (unverified)] |

The primary governance concerns are reputational (long-tenured directors, overboarding) and economic (SBC dilution without buyback offset) rather than structural (no entrenchment devices). |

**Note:** The partial-data flag (proxy not in pool) affects confidence on committee composition rows (rated 3 from web sources) but does NOT cap the board-and-shareholder-rights score for this agent — the board structure itself is readable from the 10-K and CIQ sources. The broader MODULE_RULES cap (shareholder friendliness max 60) applies to the module-level composite across agents 03, 04, and 05; this agent's individual score of 84 reflects what is knowable about structural rights, not the broader incentive/ownership picture.

## management-governance / 06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md

_Source: `06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md`_

# Candor & Disclosure Quality — AMZN

**Reporting standard:** US GAAP. **Currency:** USD. **Fiscal year end:** December 31. **Jurisdiction:** United States (NasdaqGS). **Date:** 2026-07-11.

**Partial-data note on proxy:** The 2026 DEF 14A is absent from the pool. Beneficial-ownership table, AGM voting results, and compensation tables are unavailable. These gaps do not affect the candor assessment (which rests on transcripts, shareholder letters, and filings) but are noted for completeness.

**Cross-module inputs used:** `01_management-and-track-record.md` (promise-vs-delivery, misses); `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md` (non-GAAP aggressiveness, accrual flags); `earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md` (guidance reliability history). Verbatim earnings call transcripts for Q3 2025, Q4 2025, and Q1 2026 are in the pool and used directly for tone and Q&A assessment.

---

## 1. Owning the Misses

From `01_management-and-track-record`, the misses or near-misses in the observable record are:

| Miss (from 01) | How It Was Communicated | Owned or Obscured? | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| FY2025 capex exceeded Q3 2025 guidance by $6.8B ($131.8B actual vs ~$125B guided) | CFO Olsavsky acknowledged the overage in the Q4 2025 call without formal apology but attributed it to faster-than-guided Q4 capacity additions; framed as demand acceleration rather than cost failure; quantified precisely (stated "$125B" at Q3 call, disclosed $131.8B in the 10-K); no attempt to pre-spin or bury the miss | **Owned — explicitly named and quantified, directional explanation consistent with facts** | Q3 2025 Earnings Call (Oct 30, 2025), CFO prepared remarks; FY2025 10-K, Cash Flow Statement (Apr 9, 2026) |
| Q3 2025 reported operating income of $17.4B was $4.3B below underlying performance due to FTC settlement ($2.5B) and severance ($1.8B) | Jassy opened his Q3 prepared remarks in the first paragraph: "Operating income was $17.4 billion, but would have been over $21 billion, if not for 2 special Q3 expenses, $2.5 billion for an FTC settlement and $1.8 billion for estimated severance costs." Both charges disclosed immediately, quantified, and attributed to named events. CFO repeated the breakdown in his remarks. | **Owned — disclosed in the opening sentence of CEO's prepared remarks, before any positive narrative** | Q3 2025 Earnings Call (Oct 30, 2025), Jassy prepared remarks (verbatim) |
| FCF declined from $38.2B (FY2024) to $11.2B (FY2025), and strict FCF was $7.7B; LTM Mar-26 strict FCF was negative (−$2.5B) | Management framed this explicitly in the FY2025 Shareholder Letter (pp.214–231 of annual report) and in Q4 2025 call Q&A: "Free Cash Flow decreased from $38 billion to $11 billion, driven primarily by a year-over-year increase of $50.7 billion in purchases of property and equipment." Jassy wrote in the shareholder letter: "in times of very high growth (like now), where the capex growth meaningfully outpaces the revenue growth, the early-years FCF is challenged." This language was used in FY2023 and FY2024 letters — management pre-disclosed the decline before it happened. | **Owned pre-emptively — framed transparently in prior shareholder letters as an expected and intentional outcome, then confirmed in the FY2025 letter** | FY2025 Annual Report (Apr 9, 2026), Shareholder Letter, pp.214–219; Q4 2025 Earnings Call (Feb 5, 2026), Q&A |
| AWS FY2025 growth of 19.2% was slightly below the implied >20% target from the FY2024 letter | The FY2025 shareholder letter and Q4 2025 call did not explicitly reference the prior-year >20% target; management instead pointed to Q4 2025 (24%) and Q1 2026 (28%) acceleration as the relevant trajectory. The annual full-year 19.2% miss vs. "implied >20%" was not owned directly — though the quarterly re-acceleration was prominently highlighted. | **Partially obscured — the full-year AWS miss was not acknowledged; management pivoted to Q4 and Q1 acceleration, which is defensible on substance but represents a selective presentation** | FY2024 Annual Report (Feb 2025), Shareholder Letter; Q4 2025 Earnings Call (Feb 5, 2026), prepared remarks |
| Q1 2026 EPS "beat" of +69.5% included a large Anthropic investment mark-up that inflated reported EPS by approximately $2.47/share equivalent | Management proactively disclosed the Anthropic mark in Q3 2025 CFO remarks: "our third quarter net income of $21.2 billion includes a pretax gain of $9.5 billion related to our investment in Anthropic. This investment activity is not related to Amazon's ongoing operations and is included in nonoperating income." However, management never issued a proactive adjusted EPS metric. Analysts must do the normalization themselves. The disclosure is adequate in content but management's choice to lead with GAAP EPS without a voluntary normalized figure creates a flattering optical beat each time the Anthropic mark runs positive. | **Disclosed but not proactively adjusted — the disclosure meets the filing standard; the absence of voluntary adjusted EPS is a mild style concern, not a credibility failure** | Q3 2025 Earnings Call (Oct 30, 2025), CFO prepared remarks; FY2025 10-K, Item 7, p.27; earnings/06_earnings-quality.md |

**Summary:** The record shows management owns real operational misses (FTC, severance, capex overage) clearly and early, often in the first sentence of prepared remarks. The FCF compression was pre-disclosed in prior-year letters, making it a kept promise rather than a miss. The only material presentation gap is the selective omission of the FY2025 full-year AWS miss vs. the prior implied >20% target, offset by the Q4/Q1 acceleration being genuine.

---

## 2. Non-GAAP / Adjustment Aggressiveness

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Recurring "one-off" addbacks | Amazon does NOT publish a formal adjusted EBITDA or adjusted EBIT. The company's only official non-GAAP measure is Free Cash Flow. In Q3 2025, management verbally described underlying operating income ($21.7B ex-charges) but did not issue a press-release table for it. Recurring "Other operating expense" has appeared every year ($62M FY2021, $163M FY2022, $767M FY2023, $763M FY2024, $4,639M FY2025) — treating this as perpetually "non-recurring" overstates normalized earnings. However, compared to peers who issue formal adjusted EBITDA, Amazon's restraint from publishing adjusted figures is actually LESS aggressive, not more. | Q3 2025 Earnings Call (Oct 30, 2025), CFO remarks; FY2025 10-K, Item 7, p.27 |
| GAAP-to-adjusted gap (magnitude / trend) | Largest gap: reported diluted EPS $7.17 vs normalized EPS $4.70 in FY2025 — a 34.5% difference, entirely from the $15,301M Anthropic investment gain in "Other income." Operating income (EBIT) is UNAFFECTED by this gap — it is a below-the-line item. The GAAP-to-adjusted gap for EBIT is small: FY2025 EBIT $79,975M vs. ~$84,275M ex one-offs, a 5.4% difference. The EPS gap is large and recurring in both directions (FY2021 +$14.7B; FY2022 −$15.9B; FY2025 +$15.3B). Normalized EPS $4.70 is materially lower than GAAP EPS $7.17. | earnings/06_earnings-quality.md; FY2025 10-K, Item 7, p.27; Capital IQ Income Statement: Normalized Diluted EPS $4.702 |
| Does adjusted framing flatter reality? | Management does NOT issue adjusted EPS — this is actually conservative relative to peers. The flattering presentation is the LACK of proactive normalization: management leads with "net income of $X including a pretax gain of $Y" in the transcript, which frames the investment gains as a positive headline before the disclaimer. Analysts and the financial press use GAAP EPS in headlines. This creates the appearance of large EPS beats in years with big Anthropic marks (FY2025, FY2021) and large EPS misses in years with losses (FY2022). Operating income — the clean line — was never distorted. The server useful-life flip (5→6yr Jan 2024, 6→5yr for a subset Jan 2025, heavy equipment 10→13yr Jan 2025) is a modest earnings-management concern, disclosed clearly, but two direction changes in consecutive years on the same asset class is unusual. The combined net effect across both years on the affected servers roughly offsets. | earnings/06_earnings-quality.md, Sections 4, 5, 7; FY2025 10-K, Note 1, p.41–47 |

**Assessment:** Amazon's non-GAAP posture is notably conservative by large-cap standards — no adjusted EBITDA, no adjusted EPS, no formal exclusion of SBC or restructuring. The investment-gain distortion in reported EPS is real and recurring, but it is disclosed clearly and is a GAAP accounting consequence of a large strategic investment portfolio, not a management-engineered adjustment. The server useful-life changes are the one area where the accounting-estimate choices moved earnings in convenient directions, though each was disclosed and the two-year net effect was roughly neutral.

---

## 3. Disclosure Transparency

Segment economics are disclosed in useful detail: AWS revenue, operating income, and operating margin are reported separately each quarter with sequential and year-over-year comparisons. North America and International operating income are also disclosed. One transparency gap stands out: **Amazon does not disclose the split of capital expenditures between maintenance and growth**, forcing analysts to treat all capex as equivalent — this matters significantly when $131.8B in gross capex includes both sustaining and growth components, and when management argues that FCF will recover as growth capex moderates. A second gap: **advertising revenue is reported as a single line without a regional or format breakdown** (sponsored products, DSP, video). Both of these limitations are consistent with peer practice in e-commerce/cloud and do not represent deliberate obscuring of known-bad data.

The AWS backlog ($364B at Q1 2026, up from $244B at Q4 2025) and remaining performance obligation disclosure in the 10-K Note 10 is a positive transparency signal — management discloses forward revenue commitments that provide independent evidence for the capex thesis, going beyond what most companies disclose. The FY2025 10-K also discloses total purchase obligations of $439.7B, which gives investors visibility into the full committed spend level. There is no evidence of disclosure becoming less transparent over the observable three-year period; the backlog and commitment disclosures have expanded over FY2023–FY2025 alongside the growing investment cycle.

---

## 4. Tone in Bad Times

**Q3 2025 was the worst quarter in the observable record in terms of headline miss** — reported operating income of $17.4B looked $4.3B below underlying performance due to the FTC settlement and severance. The tone in Jassy's prepared remarks was direct: the charges were named in the first sentence, quantified, and the underlying performance ($21.7B, "$1.2B above the high end of our guidance range") was then stated clearly. The CFO repeated both figures. There was no promotional language attempting to draw attention away from the headline number before disclosing the charges. Q&A on the Q3 call did not address the charges directly (no analyst pushed on FTC specifics), and management did not volunteer additional context on the settlement beyond what was in the press release. The tone was matter-of-fact, not defensive.

On the worst FCF data ($7.7B strict FCF in FY2025; negative LTM strict FCF at Q1 2026), the Q4 2025 Q&A was probed directly by Mark Mahaney (Evercore) who asked about "financial guardrails or governors" on capex. Jassy responded with a substantive answer about monetization rate and ROIC confidence, grounded in the $244B backlog and Trainium unit economics, rather than deflecting or pivoting to a generic growth narrative. He acknowledged: "the faster AWS grows, the more short-term capex we'll spend" and "we're going to invest aggressively here." This is direct acknowledgment of the FCF cost of the strategy, not cheerleading.

---

## 5. Candor Read

Management can be trusted to tell the truth when results are bad. The strongest evidence is the Q3 2025 call, where the CEO opened his remarks by immediately quantifying the $4.3B in charges and stating the underlying performance figure in the same breath — this is the opposite of spin. The FCF compression was pre-disclosed in prior-year shareholder letters before it happened, and the subsequent letters confirmed the outcome without softening it. The single most important positive signal: Amazon's shareholder letters (FY2025 pp.214–231) address the "over-hyped AI, are we in a bubble?" question directly, present the bear case against themselves ("reasonable people can disagree"), and then make an evidence-based case for their position. This is atypically candid shareholder communication for a large-cap company.

The one blemish: management does not issue voluntary adjusted EPS, which means that in years with large Anthropic mark-ups, the headline GAAP EPS beat is optically large and misleading for investors who do not read the non-operating income line carefully. This is a presentation choice management could make differently without any disclosure obligation; their peers (Alphabet, Meta) commonly issue adjusted metrics to avoid this distortion. The server useful-life flip also signals some earnings-smoothing at the margin. Neither rises to the level of a credibility concern, but both are costs to the candor grade.

**Verdict: candid / high-trust.** The biggest candor signal is that charges were disclosed in the opening sentence of the Q3 2025 prepared remarks, not buried — this is where management proves whether they tell the truth when results are bad.
